Low-nonlinearity advantage of hollow-core fiber for wavelength multiplexing of quantum entanglement and classical DWDM signals

Coexistence of classical and quantum light in optical fiber is a key requirement for scalable quantum networks. However, it is fundamentally limited by nonlinear crosstalk from intense classical signals. Hollow-core fiber (HCF), with its intrinsically low optical nonlinearity, offers a promising solution. Here, we experimentally demonstrate the coexistence of polarization-entangled photons with dense wavelength-division multiplexed (DWDM) classical signals with a total launch power of 11.2 dBm, and perform a direct comparison between equal-length single-mode fiber (SMF) and photonic-bandgap-based HCF deployed in a campus network. We observe that HCF suppresses background noise by approximately two orders of magnitude compared to SMF, while preserving high-fidelity entanglement distribution. Our results provide clear experimental evidence of the advantage of HCF and establish its potential as a transmission medium.
---
Collision-aware Bayesian mitigation of photon loss in linear optical quantum circuits

Photon loss is the dominant noise source in linear optical quantum circuits. Each lost photon turns an ideal n-photon outcome into a pattern with fewer photons, so the detector counts no longer follow the ideal output distribution, and the lossless events that do follow it become exponentially rare as the photon number grows. Discarding the lossy events (postselection) therefore wastes most of the data at realistic loss rates, and the ideal distribution has to be inferred from the lossy counts themselves. Recycling-based mitigation (Mills & Mezher, Quantum 2026; Quandela's photon_recycling) reweights lossy samples to recover lossless probabilities, but its estimators are biased and restricted to the no-collision sector, where each output mode holds at most one photon. We instead treat the raw counts as Multinomial observations under per-photon binomial loss and infer the ideal output distribution and the loss rate jointly from a Bayesian posterior, which extends naturally to outputs with collisions. On data from the independent Perceval simulator at the scale of prior work (n=4 photons in m=20 and m=10 modes), the posterior has lower KL divergence than both recycling and postselection at every tested loss rate in the no-collision benchmark. In the collision regime it recovers the loss rate and the collision-pattern probabilities, which recycling cannot estimate, and stays below recycling at every loss rate and below postselection except at low loss, where the two are comparable. Its advantage over recycling grows with the sample count, and it supplies credible intervals that the baselines lack.

Quantum Cryptanalysis on Two Feistel-Type Constructions

With the rapid advancement of quantum computing, the security of traditional cryptographic systems is facing significant challenges. As a fundamental component of symmetric cryptography, block ciphers have attracted increasing attention regarding their quantum security in recent years.
  In this paper, we propose new quantum attacks on two Feistel-type constructions, i.e., New Structure IV and the lightweight block cipher LBlock, under the quantum chosen-plaintext attack (qCPA) model. First, we construct an 8-round quantum distinguisher for New Structure IV and perform an $r(r>12)$-round quantum key-recovery attack. Compared with Li et al.'s qCPA-based result presented at INSCRYPT 2024, our attack reduces the time complexity by a factor of $2^{n/2}$, where $n$ denotes the subkey length. Second, we design a 9-round quantum distinguisher for LBlock and extend it to an $r(r>15)$-round quantum key-recovery attack. The proposed quantum distinguisher covers one more round than Liu et al.'s automated-search distinguisher at ASIACRYPT 2025, which results in a $2^{16}$ reduction in the time complexity of our $r$-round quantum key-recovery attack on LBlock. Furthermore, by analyzing the structure of the round function and the key scheduling of LBlock, we reduce the subkey guess space for the 15-round attack from 76 bits to 52 bits, lowering the time complexity from \(O(2^{38})\) to \(O(2^{26})\) by a factor of \(2^{12}\).

Engineering \(XY\) and \(ZZ\) interactions between two remote superconducting qubits via a multimode waveguide

Waveguides provide a flexible platform for long-range interactions between superconducting qubits. Here, we study how to engineer the effective \(XY\) and \(ZZ\) interactions between two qubits via qubit frequency assignments and multimode interference determined by the mode configuration and standing-wave parities of a finite multimode waveguide. For a single-mode waveguide, we find that \(XY\) and \(ZZ\) interactions cannot be simultaneously suppressed to zero and the residual qubit-qubit coupling always exists. For example, the \(XY\) interaction may be suppressed to a negligible level when two qubits and the waveguide mode satisfy the two-photon resonant condition, but the \(ZZ\) interaction cannot be switched off. For multimode configurations within the central dispersive window, the engineered interaction types are very different for odd and even numbers of modes. Even-mode configurations can readily produce strong \(XY\) interactions, whereas odd-mode configurations can strongly suppress the \(XY\) interaction while retaining a finite \(ZZ\) interaction. Full-Hamiltonian simulations of minimal two- and three-mode configurations confirm \(XY\)-dominated SWAP dynamics and \(ZZ\)-dominated CZ-type dynamics, respectively. These results establish a frequency assignment criterion of qubits and waveguide modes for selectively engineering long-range qubit-qubit interaction types induced by the waveguide with finite multimode.

Systematic Truncated Wigner Approximation for Open Spin Systems Reveals Long-Range Order Far from Equilibrium

Long-range interactions can protect dissipative order against fluctuations, but determining when they do so in two dimensions remains out of reach for existing many-body methods. We develop a continuous SU(2)-covariant truncated-Wigner framework that maps the problem onto stochastic dynamics with one Bloch sphere per site. Derived systematically from the exact Stratonovich-Weyl evolution of the Lindblad equation, it generates both drift and reservoir noise for arbitrary spin $S$. Benchmarks against exact and tensor-network calculations validate its description of non-equilibrium dynamics and steady states. Applied to 400-site two-dimensional dissipative anisotropic XY lattices, our method reveals physics missed by mean-field and perturbative approaches: vortex-antivortex proliferation destroys the predicted staggered-XY phase for nearest-neighbour coupling, whereas algebraically decaying interactions stabilize an extended $\mathbb{Z}_2$-ordered phase. We obtain the first beyond-mean-field steady-state phase diagram of the long-range interacting model, revealing reentrant loss of order at strong coupling and closure of the ordered region beyond $α\approx 3.5$. Our new method provides a scalable route to strongly correlated driven-dissipative systems with long-range interactions, higher-dimensional geometries, spin-boson couplings, and spins beyond one half, relevant to modern experiments with magnetic atoms and polar molecules in optical lattices and tweezer arrays.

Steering of topological criticality in the Su-Schrieffer-Heeger chain via non-Hermitian substitutional impurities

We investigate the topological phase in a one-dimensional Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chain in the presence of a finite concentration of non-Hermitian substitutional impurities. Our primary focus is the discovery of a deterministic mechanism to actively reconfigure the topological phase boundaries by introducing site-dependent, non-reciprocal intracell and intercell hopping amplitudes. Specifically, breaking the internal structural symmetry of the non-reciprocal impurity hoppings induces a targeted macroscopic displacement of the critical point. This mechanism allows for the controlled expansion or compression of the topological phase strictly via local defect asymmetry. We validate this boundary displacement through finite-size scaling analysis. The extracted correlation length critical exponents ($ν\approx 1.455$ and $ν\approx 1.397$ for symmetric and asymmetric configurations, respectively) confirm a disorder-induced departure from the pristine SSH universality class, with the exponent diverging at high defect densities. We also report an impurity-induced real-to-complex spectral transition geometrically characterized by the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect. Ultimately, our results establish how isolated non-Hermitian asymmetries cooperatively interact to deterministically shift macroscopic topological protection.

Field demonstration of a self-contained 10 km mobile-to-ground quantum network node via common-aperture co-propagation

In this work, we demonstrate a dual-function, common-aperture free-space optical system that simultaneously supports decoy-state BB84 quantum key distribution (QKD) at 803 nm and bidirectional classical laser communication at 640/660 nm over a 10.31 km terrestrial link from a mobile platform to a stationary ground station. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first field demonstration of a fully integrated common-aperture architecture where a co-propagating classical optical link simultaneously performs full QKD post-processing and ecrypted payload transmission over a 10 km atmospheric channel. The classical link utilizes Manchester-coded on-off keying (MC-OOK) at 10 MHz, achieving a packet error rate of $\sim$ 0.4 $\%$ (estimated bit error rate (BER) of 3.13 $\times$ 10$^{-5}$) with hardware-based self-synchronization and error-packet retransmission. Over the 10.31 km link, the QKD system achieved an average quantum bit error rate (QBER) of 2.512 $\%$ and an average secret key rate of 644 bps using rate-adaptive low-density parity-check (LDPC) codes. Finally, using the distilled keys, real-time AES-256 encrypted image transmission was executed over the co-aligned classical optical channel, proving the feasibility of a completely self-contained, jam resistant mobile quantum optical network.

Quantum algorithm for high-dimensional nonlinear stochastic differential equations based on Carleman linearization

Addressing nonlinear numerical problems in quantum computing remains challenging, but several approaches, including Carleman linearization (CL), have been proposed to construct quantum algorithms for such problems. In this work, we develop a CL-based method for a high-dimensional nonlinear stochastic differential equation (SDE) $\mathrm{d} \mathbf{X}_t = \left(A(t) \mathbf{X}_t + F(t) \mathbf{X}_t^{\otimes s}\right) \mathrm{d} t + B(t) \mathrm{d} \mathbf{W}_t$ for an $\mathbb{R}^N$-valued random process $\mathbf{X}_t$, aiming at quantum speed-up with respect to the dimension $N$. We introduce auxiliary processes $\mathbf{Y}^j_t$ that approximate $\mathbf{X}_t^{\otimes j}$, derive a linearized system of SDEs for them, and obtain the linear ordinary differential equation (ODE) governing their expectations. We then apply a quantum ODE solver to it and, from the resulting quantum state, extract an estimate for the expectation of a linear function of $\mathbf{X}_t$ by overlap estimation. We show that, provided the nonlinear and noise terms in the SDE are sufficiently small relative to the linear term, the CL error decreases exponentially with the truncation order $J$ in CL, and we can estimate the target expectation with a ${\rm polylog}(N)$ number of queries to unitaries encoding coefficient matrices $A$, $F$, and $B$. Finally, we also run a numerical demonstration for an SDE related to Langevin dynamics, observing the exponential decay of the CL error as $J$ increases.

Non-Classicality in $tW$ system via negativity and geometric quantum discord

We perform a phenomenological study of non-classical correlations in the mixed-dimensional qubit-qutrit system realized by leading order $pp\to tW$ production at $\sqrt{s}=13$ TeV in the leptonic final state. The two missing neutrinos are reconstructed with conditional normalizing flows trained on simulated detector-level objects, and the joint spin density matrix $ρ_{tW}\in\mathcal{H}_2\otimes\mathcal{H}_3$ is obtained in a generalized Bloch representation from the angular distributions of the leptonic decay products. Non-classical correlations are quantified using the Peres--Horodecki negativity and geometric quantum discord. We quantify the correlations at different region of $m_{tW}$ at the reconstruction level within the Standard Model and the impact of two reducible backgrounds viz. $t\bar{t}$ and $WWj$ processes, tagged using boosted decision trees. We further study the behavior of correlations in presence of anomalous chromomagnetic and chromoelectric dipole moments of the top quark. The sensitivity reach of the quantum observables along with polarization and spin correlation parameters are reported for current LHC and future HL-LHC scenario.

Choi-Jamiołkowski isomorphism(s) and channel/state duality, naturally!

Provided a maximally entangled state vector in ${\mathcal{H}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}$, the Choi-Jamiolkowski isomorphism of quantum information theory (or channel/state duality) produces a mapping of ${\mathsf CP}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}})$ (completely positive maps of density operators on ${\mathcal{H}}$ tothose on ${\mathcal{K}}$) onto the density operators on ${\mathcal{K}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}$, which extends to a unitary map. Here, ${\mathcal{H}}$ and ${\mathcal{K}}$ are {finite-dimensional} Hilbert spaces.The nature of this construction, the form of the associated formulas, and the possibility of versions which do not require an entangled vector seed, are illuminated from the perspective of {naturality}, in the sense of Eilenberg and MacLane.There is a unique such correspondence (``Choi map'') natural in both ${\mathcal{H}}$ and ${\mathcal{K}}$ which produces density operators on either ${\mathsf L}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{K}})$ (Hilbert-Schmidt inner product) or ${\mathcal{K}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}^*$ instead of ${\mathcal{K}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}$. In particular, no entangled state input is required. There are no such binatural maps carried on ${\mathcal{K}}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}$, but those natural in only ${\mathcal{K}}$ are canonically associated with unitary maps ${\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}}^*$ and derive from the binatural maps. The construction recovers the standard Choi-Jamiolkowski formulae and shows how they express naturality.

Quantum-Classical Hybrid Methods for Optimization: Lessons from Classical Decomposition

Quantum and physics-inspired solvers provide specialized sampling interfaces for Ising and quadratic unconstrained binary optimization models, but constrained optimization requires broader coordination and certification. This survey uses classical decomposition to analyze how such solvers can be integrated into hybrid quantum-classical algorithms. We distinguish formal decomposition, reformulation and reduction, and computational orchestration. For each interface, we ask what the transformation preserves, which assumptions make the outer method converge, and which certificates the implemented inner routine supplies. The central questions are whether that information meets the classical algorithm's requirements and whether hybridization improves solution quality or total cost enough to justify its coordination overhead. Benders decomposition, column generation, graph reduction, and copositive methods illustrate this distinction by separating heuristic proposals from classical certification. We also distinguish component effects within a fixed wrapper, reduction--solver interactions, and comparisons between complete quantum-enabled and classical systems. The analyzed studies provide conditional component benefits and favorable co-design evidence, but do not establish transferable end-to-end superiority over strong classical comparators. The resulting framework identifies opportunities for constraint programming and operations research in structure-preserving design, safeguarded hybrid algorithms, and controlled benchmarking.

Assignment maps for arbitrary reduced quantum dynamics

Given an open quantum system $S$ with environment $E$, we establish a one-to-one correspondence between completely positive trace-preserving (CPTP) maps from $S$ to $E$ and assignment maps initializing the composite system $SE$ in a possibly correlated state prior to unitary evolution. We show that every possible joint state $ρ_{SE}$ can be realized by such assignment maps, which are inherently Hermiticity-preserving and trace-preserving (HPTP). While these maps sacrifice global positivity, we derive a simple necessary and sufficient condition for any local state of $S$ to belong to their domain of validity. Furthermore, we prove that the geometry of this domain is governed by the joint state assigned to the maximally mixed input, ensuring that a non-zero volume of admissible local states can readily coexist with initial system-environment entanglement. Finally, we provide an operational protocol for witnessing such limits of validity.

Shot Allocation Strategies in Debugging Quantum Circuits

Debugging quantum programs remains challenging due to the nature of quantum measurement. As quantum algorithms and systems scale, it is increasingly crucial to develop rigorous debugging and verification approaches. Statistical assertion-based debugging validates quantum program behavior through repeated circuit execution and statistical hypothesis testing, but existing frameworks treat shot count as a fixed input parameter, leaving the problem of optimal shot allocation across multiple mid-circuit assertions unexplored. To improve debugging efficiency by making better use of given shot counts, this paper proposes and evaluates four shot allocation strategies within the QDB quantum debugger implemented within the CUDA-Q framework. These allocation approaches: equal split, static weight allocation, adaptive entropy routing, and sequential retirement, use statistical approaches to test whether qubits have expected state characteristics at different points in execution. We evaluate these allocation strategies on different circuits, under both noise-free and depolarizing noise conditions. We show how adaptive strategies use shots efficiently by concentrating them on assertions with high uncertainty. This substantially reduces total shot usage while maintaining comparable debugging confidence.

Orchestrating Key Scarcity: Global Capacity Allocation in Hybrid QKD-PQC Networks

This article presents a Distributed Key Management System (DKMS) that provisions quantum-safe symmetric encryption keys to applications across a network. To overcome the distance and rate limitations inherent to Quantum Key Distribution (QKD), the architecture adopts a hybrid design: it utilizes multi-hop trusted relays for physical key transport, while applying an end-to-end Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) layer to ensure the relayed key material remains strictly protected from intermediate nodes. Because QKD transforms key generation into a scarce physical resource, managing unpredictable application demands poses a critical capacity allocation problem. The core contribution of this work is an SDN-driven control plane that globally orchestrates the network. Rather than relying on vulnerable hop-by-hop capacity reservations, a central controller simultaneously computes optimal filling rates and multipath routing for all per-peer key buffers. This global orchestration ensures that no application is starved and that all key buffers synchronize their filling processes. Evaluated on a Kubernetes-based digital twin across five distinct network topologies, the controller successfully filled all key buffers to capacity in under six minutes. Furthermore, under deliberately excessive demand, the network demonstrated graceful degradation, safely rejecting excess requests through controlled rate-limiting rather than experiencing catastrophic failure.

Quantum-Seeded Distributed Post-Quantum Group Signatures for 5G V2X Communications

Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) communications rely on elliptic-curve signatures that a quantum adversary breaks and on pseudonym certificates whose anonymity rests on rotation rate, not cryptography. This work presents qDGSP, a post-quantum V2X infrastructure: a hash-based dynamic group signature scheme (DGSP, over SPHINCS+ and WOTS+) distributed between a central controller and roadside verifiers. Group keys derive deterministically from quantum key distribution (QKD) seeds served over the ETSI GS QKD 014 interface, so no group-signature secret crosses the classical channel. qDGSP-EA (Ephemeral Association) binds an ephemeral ML-DSA-65 key to one group signature per 5G cell: messages remain unlinkable across cells, linkable within one. On a testbed with emulated 5G links, median end-to-end latency stayed within the 100ms ETSI budget for every variant. The qDGSP-EA intra-cell DENM matched the NIST ML-DSA-65 baseline on the wire, and under load EA mode raised delivery from roughly 78% to 93%.

Counterexamples to the total-current Hohenberg-Kohn conjecture

The total-current Hohenberg-Kohn conjecture asserts that the ground-state particle density and total current determine the scalar potential up to an additive constant and the vector potential up to a gauge transformation. We give three counterexamples for two spinless fermions in the physically relevant setting of three spatial dimensions. In each, a Hamiltonian $H^{v,\mathbf{A}}$ has a nondegenerate ground state with identically zero total current but nonzero magnetic field. Complex conjugation then yields a second Hamiltonian $H^{v,-\mathbf{A}}$, with the same scalar potential and interaction, whose ground state has the same density and the same zero current, while the magnetic field is reversed. The first two examples are noninteracting. One is explicit, with a smooth bounded vector potential and a Gaussian density. The other has smooth bounded scalar and vector potentials in the standard potential spaces. The third example has Coulomb repulsion with unit coupling, an isotropic harmonic scalar potential, and Dirac monopole fields of magnetic charge $\pm 1/2$. The monopole example uses vector potentials defined on separate spatial patches, since these fields are not curls of globally defined smooth vector potentials.

Optimal four-copy detection of the three-tangle and its fault-tolerant cost

The three-tangle, $τ_3$, is a measure of genuine tripartite entanglement that distinguishes GHZ-type from W-type entanglement in pure three-qubit states. Here, we identify the statistically optimal one-sided four-copy detector of the three-tangle and study its implementation cost. The optimal detector has click probability $τ_3^2/48$, while previous four-copy constructions achieve $τ_3^2/256$ and therefore require asymptotically $16/3$ times as many experimental repetitions. Using the representation-theoretic structure of the problem, we construct an ancilla-free implementation of the optimal detector using 15 CNOT gates, together with an 11-CNOT detector with click probability $τ_3^2/96$. These results reveal a tradeoff between statistical efficiency and implementation cost, since reducing the number of repetitions requires increasingly expensive circuits. We then show that this tradeoff persists once the circuits are encoded. In a fully fault-tolerant setting, the increased click probability comes with a substantial non-Clifford synthesis cost, while an early fault-tolerant implementation based on $T$ gates and STAR rotations reduces this overhead at the expense of additional encoded resources. We quantify these costs using both the Steane and $[[8,3,2]]$ codes, providing explicit resource estimates for implementing the optimal detector across different computational regimes.

Controlling Optical Quantum Interference with Plasmonic Metasurfaces

The ability to efficiently control optical quantum interference by the same device is a challenging task that promises to enable key operations in quantum information processing. Here, we tackle this problem by presenting a composite quantum plasmonic metasurface operating as an ultrathin non-unitary beamsplitter capable of achieving both single photon bosonic bunching and fermionic anti-bunching interference just by varying the incident photons frequency or changing the design dimensions. The presented metasurface combines a plasmonic grating corrugated by periodic nanoslits that can achieve extraordinary optical transmission with additional nanostripes on top forming nanocavities characterized by localized gap plasmon resonances. The photon-photon interactions are analyzed based on calculating the quantum coalescence metric which accurately portrays the obtained bunching or anti-bunching quantum interference. The presented quantum interference operation is applicable to both fundamental non-classical light interactions and in the design of more applied quantum optical logic gates used in quantum computing and communications.

Comparative Optical Spectroscopy of Er$^{3+}$, Tm$^{3+}$, and Eu$^{3+}$ Molecular Complexes at Cryogenic Temperature

Rare-earth molecular complexes are attracting increasing interest for quantum information applications because of their structural flexibility and chemical tunability. Here, we compare the cryogenic optical properties of Er$^{3+}$, Tm$^{3+}$, and Eu$^{3+}$ complexes in the same ligand environment. Optical spectroscopy shows differing behavior across the three systems: spectral hole burning is observed in the Eu$^{3+}$ complex, whereas no measurable hole is detected in the Er$^{3+}$ and Tm$^{3+}$ complexes. We measure laser-induced local heating and rethermalization in the Er$^{3+}$ and Tm$^{3+}$ powder-like samples, observing strong local heating and slow thermal relaxation in the cold-plate cryostat. The recovery occurs over several seconds and, for some Er$^{3+}$ excitation conditions, extends over tens of seconds. The comparison across the three ions nevertheless suggests that heating alone is unlikely to account for the full contrast. Rather, the different behaviors of Eu$^{3+}$, Tm$^{3+}$, and Er$^{3+}$ point to ion-dependent relaxation and decoherence processes associated with their electronic level structure and coupling to the molecular environment. Temperature-dependent linewidth and line-shift measurements are further consistent with phonon-mediated broadening through coupling to crystal-field levels.

Quantum Fluctuations and Wigner Dynamics of a Carbon Nanotube Oscillator Driven by an Atomic Force Microscope

Suspended carbon nanotubes provide a useful platform for studying controlled nanomechanical motion in regimes where environmental fluctuations, memory effects, and quantum coherence can all influence the dynamics. We model the fundamental flexural mode of a carbon nanotube driven by an atomic force microscope as a harmonic quantum Brownian oscillator subject to a prescribed classical force and coupled linearly to a thermal environment. Starting from the generalized Langevin equation, we formulate the reduced dynamics directly in phase space, where the Wigner function evolves through a Gaussian propagator determined by dissipative linear transport, a deterministic force-induced displacement, and bath-induced fluctuations. For a Drude memory kernel, the retarded response is expressed through the three roots of a characteristic cubic equation. In the classical-noise limit, the three independent entries of the finite-time covariance matrix reduce to explicit finite sums over these response poles, eliminating numerical double-time covariance integration. We extend the construction to the full quantum fluctuation spectrum, obtaining a thermal frequency series representation that retains zero-point and low-temperature fluctuations. For an initial two-packet superposition, we examine interference attenuation and the integrated negative part of the Wigner function, which provides a phase space measure of nonclassicality. These results provide an explicit analytical framework for finite-memory quantum Brownian motion of bound oscillators and a tractable phase space description of driven carbon nanotube dynamics relevant to future quantum state preparation and quantum tomography experiments.

The next phase of quantum energy science: from mechanisms to applications

A growing research community spanning quantum batteries, molecular and excitonic energy transport, polaritonics, quantum materials and nuclear control has begun to describe its work in terms of quantum energy or quantum energy science. What connects these otherwise disparate efforts is the deliberate use of quantum states, couplings, collective effects and engineered environments to control energetic states and pathways. A key difference from quantum information science is that the complete quantum state does not always need to be preserved; engineered dissipation or dephasing can even improve the desired outcome. We organize this emerging domain through a framework that connects mechanisms, functions, capabilities, and applications. We identify five elementary functions (Excite, Hold, Release, Transfer, and Transform) and examine the mechanisms that implement them. The same functions recur from the molecular to the nuclear scale, although their relative implementation difficulty can differ sharply. Moving toward technology, we highlight three engineering capabilities (Storage, Routing, and Conversion) through the performance metrics that matter for their applications. Stretch scenarios illustrate what performance targets could make these capabilities technologically consequential.

Allight: A Perfect Photon Collector

Collecting photons from quantum emitters remains a key limitation in light-matter interaction because conventional lenses collect light only from one direction. Here, we introduce a perfect light collector by inverse-designing a surrounding dielectric structure that encloses the emitter to increase photon-collection efficiency toward unity. Decomposing the device into several refractive interfaces enables emission collection from the full 4$π$ solid angle with adiabatic reshaping into a collimated beam. We experimentally demonstrate, in two dimensions, the ray bending that collimates omnidirectional emission. Our collector transforms current paradigms for highly efficient photon collection from narrowband, cavity-based techniques into experimentally robust, broadband, non-resonant dielectric devices.

Active isolation of transmon readout with a tunable inductive bridge

Scalable superconducting quantum computation requires fast, high-fidelity readout. Currently, this is assisted through cryogenic amplification of the readout signal. In traditional dispersive readout schemes, the amplifiers that are used to provide high signal-to-noise ratio also generate thermal noise that can decohere the qubits, demanding additional ferrite-based isolators to suppress this noise. Here, we integrate a chip-scale microwave switch, the tunable inductive bridge, into the readout chain of a fixed-frequency transmon. Flux-pulsing the switch, such that it transmits only during the readout, did not make a discernable difference to qubit coherence, residual excited-state population, or readout fidelity metrics. By injecting broadband noise that mimics amplifier thermal photons, we show that the switch suppresses the resulting qubit dephasing by more than $30$~dB, consistent with its independently measured on-off ratio, establishing it as a potential chip-based integrated replacement for readout-chain isolators.

A group theoretic calculation of multi-invariants of some stabilizer states

We study multi-invariants---a multi-partite generalization of Renyi entropies---of simple stabilizer states, for an arbitrary replica symmetry group. We develop a group theoretic technique for evaluating such invariants, which operates on the replica group and involves counting intersections among its cosets. Our techniques streamline the calculations of multi-invariants for graph states with two GHZ-like nodes and for certain generalizations thereof. The methodology developed here is most suitable when the quantum state is simple but flexibility over the replica group is desired.

Rényi multi-entropy of graph states and the $n -> 1$ limit

Rényi multi-entropy extends the Rényi entropy to pure states of more than two parties. It is defined by a replica contraction at integer replica number $n$, and the multi-entropy is its $n\to1$ limit. In holography this limit is conjectured to compute the area of a minimal soap-film web in the bulk. Understanding multi-entropy for general states, however, is harder. Unlike the bipartite case, the replica contraction is not a moment of a density matrix, and the continuation to $n=1$ has to be found by other means. We study this problem for qubit graph states. We show that at every odd $n$ the contraction equals $2^{-k/2}$, where $k$ is a sum of ranks of matrices over a finite field, one row and one column per qubit. We define a class of graphs that we call collision-controlled, for which the rank sum is a polynomial in $n$. This class includes graphs without even cycles and all complete bipartite graphs. The natural polynomial $n \to 1$ continuation is a finite sum over partitions of the parties. While the Rényi multi-entropy is non-increasing under single-qubit Pauli measurements at every odd integer $n>1$, the continuation to $n=1$ is not. A $Z$ measurement on one qubit of an odd ring with $2r + 1 \geq 7$ qubits raises it from $(r+1)\log 2$ to $(2r-1) \log 2$. Thus the continuation is not an entanglement monotone.

Cavity models of non-Abelian gauge theories

In quantum optics and related fields, cavity quantum electrodynamics provides a powerful framework for modeling the low-energy properties of bound charges coupled to electromagnetic fields, using a minimal set of atomic levels and isolated photonic modes. Here, we develop a similar framework for non-Abelian gauge theories, focusing on the pure-gauge dynamics of SU(2) fields as the conceptually simplest example. Starting from the corresponding Yang-Mills Lagrangian, we derive an SU(2) cavity Hamiltonian in the Coulomb gauge, represented in terms of only three localized bosonic modes. Unlike its non-interacting electromagnetic counterpart, this model exhibits a nontrivial excitation spectrum featuring bound composite gauge particles ('glueballs') as the lowest-energy configurations. We extend this framework to one-dimensional arrays of coupled SU(2) cavities and discuss the driven excitation of a glueball condensate and the clustering of multi-glueball states as two elementary interaction-induced processes in these models. These examples offer a glimpse into the multitude of novel many-body phenomena that arise within this cavity SU(2) framework and we show how this physics at the interface between particle physics and quantum optics can be experimentally explored using state-of-the-art superconducting quantum circuits.

Polynomial-Time Classical Simulation of BosonSampling under Any Constant-Fraction Photon Loss

Understanding how photon loss changes the classical complexity of BosonSampling is central to assessing the robustness of near-term photonic quantum advantage. In this work, we present a polynomial-time classical simulation algorithm for lossy BosonSampling that applies whenever a constant fraction of the input photons survives. We consider the standard fixed-loss model, where $N$ input photons propagate through an $M$-mode interferometer and exactly $N-n$ photons are lost uniformly at random, leaving $n$ surviving photons at the output. For any constant surviving fraction $n/N\in(0,1)$, the algorithm samples from a distribution within total variation distance $\varepsilon$ of the fixed-loss BosonSampling distribution with probability at least $1-δ$ over Haar-random interferometers, in time $\operatorname{poly}(M,N,\varepsilon^{-1},δ^{-1})$. We further extend the fixed-loss analysis to the uniform-transmission-loss model and obtain analogous polynomial-time simulability for any fixed transmission rate $η\in(0,1)$. These results substantially extend the known provably simulable regime of $n=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon N})$ surviving photons or $η=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon/N})$ transmission rate, and demonstrate that even the experimentally relevant linear-survival regime can admit efficient classical simulation under the conditions considered here.

Parallel Omnidirectional Atom Rearrangement with Dynamic Holograms

The ability to assemble and reconfigure individual atoms is central to performing quantum science using optical tweezer arrays. Here, we demonstrate parallel omnidirectional atom transport and rearrangement using dynamic holograms generated by a single kilohertz-rate spatial light modulator. By carefully characterizing survival and heating, we show that atoms remain confined even when successive holograms are commanded before the liquid crystal has settled, enabling significantly faster transport. To generate holograms in real time, we develop and implement a warm-start, weighted Gerchberg-Saxton algorithm that achieves performance comparable to neural-network methods without requiring any training. Combining these techniques, we assemble ytterbium atoms into a 2078-site kagome lattice with 99.0% mean filling. Our approach is readily adaptable to existing tweezer platforms and provides a method for large-scale SLM-based atom assembly as well as programmable three-dimensional control.

Spectral signatures of hidden order and hidden quantum critical points

Hidden-order phases elude the conventional Ginzburg-Landau framework: although they spontaneously break symmetry, their order remains invisible to standard local two-point correlators. Instead, they are typically characterized by nonlocal (string) order parameters and are believed to underlie a range of exotic quantum states, such as symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases. While hidden correlations have previously been detected through nonlocal observables in quantum gas microscope experiments, in realistic solid-state systems multipoint observables are typically inaccessible and the signatures of hidden order remain largely unexplored. In this work, we show that hidden order in doped spin chains can be directly probed through dynamical two-point response functions measurable by resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS) and neutron scattering. Focusing on a minimal model, we numerically identify clear spectral fingerprints of both the hidden-order phase, its associated hidden quantum critical point as well as a specific group of gapless SPT phases. Complementing our numerical results with analytical insights, we uncover the microscopic origin of these spectral signatures and demonstrate how they emerge from the interplay between spin and charge degrees of freedom. Together, our results establish spectroscopy as a practical probe of hidden order and gapless SPT phases and provide a unified understanding of the low-energy physics governing these unconventional quantum phases.

Nambu-Goldstone Bosons and Quantum Fisher Information

The quantum Fisher matrix and geometric tensor are powerful tools for quantifying the nature of quantum systems. Unfortunately, the formalism needed to compute these quantities is opaque and has limited the development of physical intuition for their behavior, even in the ground state. For quantum systems with continuous symmetries, we know the ground state can be understood in a model-independent and intuitive way from the correlation functions of conserved currents and charges. In this paper, we demonstrate an equivalence between the quantum Fisher matrix associated with the ground states of systems with continuous symmetries and the Nambu-Goldstone. bosons produced by broken generators. We will show that the information metric defined by the Fisher matrix is the same as the Zamolodchikov metric that defines the coset space of the system. Next we show that the Uhlmann curvature associated with multi-parameter estimation is the same as the charge density matrix that defines the difference between type-A and type-B Nambu-Goldstone bosons. We demonstrate these connections in several examples and discuss the generalization to finite temperature.

Boundary-Sensitive Quantum Order on Non-Amenable Lattices

We show that quantum systems on non-amenable lattices, whose boundaries constitute an extensive fraction of the system, host novel forms of bulk ordering with no Euclidean counterpart. Combining boundary-retaining iDMRG with an exact symmetry-fixing duality, we investigate $p<5$ state clock models on the coordination-3 infinite Cayley tree. We find that between the ordered and disordered phases lies an emergent intermediate phase with bulk order induced by an arbitrarily weak boundary field rather than by conventional spontaneous symmetry-breaking. This establishes boundary-sensitive order as a distinct quantum ordering mechanism defying Landau's spontaneous symmetry-breaking paradigm. The intermediate phase realizes a bulk-boundary correspondence in which the unpolarized boundary selects a specific ordered bulk state.

Quantum algorithms for optimal control: large-scale finite-horizon linear-quadratic regulation

We develop quantum algorithms for optimal control over a prescribed finite time interval (finite horizon), focusing on continuous-time linear-quadratic regulation (LQR). This foundational control problem often requires solving a nonlinear matrix Riccati equation, which can be costly for large systems and long control durations on conventional classical solvers. We instead work directly with the state-costate optimality equations, reformulating the LQR problem as a multiple-shooting linear system. Importantly, we prove a condition-number bound for the system that is independent of the horizon, under stabilizability and detectability assumptions. By incorporating the initial and final conditions into a single linear system, we can solve the subsequent linear system more efficiently with a quantum linear systems subroutine. For a given initial state, our quantum algorithms return classical estimates of the optimal cost and the optimal control inputs at a selected time, without constructing a Riccati solution operator. Given sparse-matrix block-encodings, an initial-state preparation oracle and a fixed number of control inputs, the query and gate complexities scale polylogarithmically with system size and control duration, provided that sparsity, relevant norms, and stability parameters are bounded. The dependence on the target error is inverse-linear up to logarithmic factors. We establish the required bounds uniformly in system size and duration for a family of damped mass-spring chains with two endpoint controls. Numerical experiments support the analysis.

Supervised Learning with Quantum Hamiltonian Embeddings

Machine learning is widely regarded as a promising application in which quantum computing could offer a computational advantage. However, general quantum machine learning models with provable guarantees on performance, trainability, and efficient inference remain limited. In this work, we introduce quantum Hamiltonian embeddings, a supervised learning framework in which a classical training dataset is encoded into a single effective, parameterized Hamiltonian. Training optimizes the parameters of this data-dependent Hamiltonian, whose approximate ground state defines the classification rule. Once the ground state has been approximated using a quantum algorithm, inference can be performed either classically using a classical representation of the learned state, or with a quantum representation of the state. We establish efficient methods for gradient estimation and prove favorable generalization guarantees. Furthermore, under standard cryptographic assumptions, we show that Hamiltonian embeddings can realize hypothesis functions beyond the reach of efficient classical algorithms. We then demonstrate the framework at scale on IBM Heron processors using a standard benchmark dataset. Together, these results establish Hamiltonian embeddings as a new framework for quantum machine learning, combining theoretical guarantees with a path for experimental investigations at scale on quantum hardware.

Fast Hubbard-Thouless Pumping with Local Counterdiabatic Driving

Counterdiabatic (CD) driving suppresses the excitations of fast protocols by adding an auxiliary Hamiltonian, but in many-body and topological systems the exact CD term is generally non-local and multi-body. We study this obstruction for Thouless pumping in the Rice-Mele model, first without and then with interactions. For noninteracting fermions, the exact CD term of the pump is non-local, but the variational framework of Sels and Polkovnikov yields accurate nearest-neighbor approximations. For the spinful Rice-Mele chain with onsite and nearest-neighbor repulsion, in the regime where the competition of the two interactions stabilizes quantized pumping, this approach fails: the infinite-temperature variational action only penalizes the CD amplitudes, and the resulting two-parameter nearest-neighbor term does not prevent the pump from breaking down when the cycle time is reduced from $50/t$ to $2/t$. We trace the failure to the structure of the exact many-body gauge potential, whose amplitude for each hop depends on the interaction energy that the hop costs. Resolving the nearest-neighbor currents by this energy, and fixing the coefficients with a ground-state-weighted action, gives a local ansatz with 14 coefficients that keeps the interacting pump quantized for cycle times $T=2/t$ and $T=1/t$, with an accuracy per site that does not degrade between rings of 8 and 12 sites.

Correlated decoding loses the surface code's effective distance below threshold

Correlated decoding improves matching and clustering surface-code decoders by reweighting one decoding subgraph conditioned on the other's correction. We show that standard reweighting can cause correlated decoders to fail on size-$(d-1)/2$ fault configurations that a distance-$d$ code should correct, reducing error suppression deep below threshold. Under circuit-level noise, a single first-pass edge can discount several correlated edges, even though their joint occurrence requires additional independent faults, so the decoder favors an over-discounted correction. Regularizing the reweighting rule restores the nominal distance scaling and a clear correlated-decoding advantage.

High-Accuracy Propagation-Assisted Local Decoding for Fast Transversal Clifford Circuits

We benchmark the Iterative Redecode Scheme (IRS) on fast transversal Clifford circuits, achieving high-accuracy correlated decoding using local memory-style detectors and decoders. On a deep random Clifford benchmark, IRS yields approximately $4\times$ lower logical failure rates than the ghost protocol at $d=11$ with matched buffers, and $15\times$ lower rates than logical observable matching (LOM). In three-block GHZ preparation, IRS achieves $2\times$ lower logical error rates than reliable logical Pauli-product decoding with matched initialisation, improving to $25\times$ at $d=19$ with $\lceil d/2\rceil$ full initialisation rounds. We place IRS, which builds on Wan et al.'s scheme, within a broader propagation-assisted local (PAL) framework, where local decoders exchange messages rather than decoding a global hypergraph. IRS alternates local minimum-weight perfect matching (MWPM) on memory-equivalent detector error models (DEMs) with Pauli-frame propagation and detector updates across transversal CNOT layers, seeking local error estimates consistent with the global detector record. Prior-informed DEMs account for probabilities of propagated errors missing from the memory-equivalent model, improving initial decoding and reducing logical error rates. Zero-weight physical Pauli mechanisms enable inference and propagation of initially indeterminate stabilizer outcomes. Error rates saturate after a few iterations even for deep, adversarial alternating-tCNOT circuits, allowing iteration caps with negligible loss of accuracy. We find syndrome-extraction buffers of order $\lfloor d/4\rfloor$ sufficient for distance-dependent error suppression in the demanding regimes studied. The approach extends to other local decoders and CSS codes; for repeated logical $S$ gates, IRS achieves approximately $4$ to $10\times$ lower logical error rates than LOM, depending on initialisation.

PPT-assisted capacities of bosonic Gaussian channels

We determine the exact quantum communication and entanglement-distribution capacities of three fundamental bosonic channels, namely thermal loss, thermal amplification and additive Gaussian noise, assisted by operations that completely preserve positive partial transpose (PPT). These operations are more powerful than local operations and classical communication (LOCC), but cannot generate distillable entanglement on their own. For thermal loss, the unconstrained capacity coincides with the Pirandola-Laurenza-Ottaviani-Banchi (PLOB) bound throughout the non-entanglement-breaking regime. The corresponding upper bounds are also attained for thermal amplification and additive noise. We extend these results to energy-constrained communication, establishing exact capacities for all three channels at every prescribed mean input energy. Our results solve a class of bosonic capacity problems under PPT assistance and establish exact benchmarks for determining whether the same communication rates can be achieved via LOCC alone.

Phase-sensitive quantum-state texture during thermalization of a Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya Heisenberg dimer

We investigate quantum-state texture during the thermal relaxation of an anisotropic spin-$1/2$ Heisenberg dimer subject to a longitudinal magnetic field and a Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM) interaction. Starting from local transverse and longitudinal couplings to a bosonic bath, we construct a global weak-coupling Lindblad equation, identify its transition channels, and discuss the applicability and limitations of the secular approximation. The Gibbs state is obtained explicitly in the local spin basis, revealing that the DM coupling controls both the modulus and phase of the single-excitation coherence by modifying the energy splitting and rotating the chiral phase. For heating and cooling protocols, we compare rugosity, fidelity-based texture, and trace-distance texture with coherence, vector spin chirality, and purity. The relaxation dynamics separates population thermalization from the decay and precession of deliberately prepared energy-basis coherence. Their combined contribution to local coherence produces DM-dependent texture overshoots and, during cooling, damped oscillations and sign changes of the coherence quadratures. These transients differ from the evolution of coherence magnitude and purity, while trace-distance texture weights them differently from the overlap-based quantifiers. Calculations at reduced dissipation preserve the texture maxima within the secular model. The results identify a phase-sensitive diagnostic of relaxation.

Measurement-efficient energy estimator for quantum chemistry of photodynamic materials

Accurate modelling of chemical reaction rates requires precise correlated energy differences. Quantum computing offers a potential route to their determination, but extracting energies from quantum-prepared states can demand prohibitive sampling. We demonstrate measurement-efficient correlated energy estimation using an electronically challenging H$_2$DBB--Ru model derived from a Ru-functionalized hafnium metal-organic framework relevant to radiodynamic and photodynamic therapies. The mixed estimator combines a quantum-prepared target with sparse multiconfiguration references and recovers ground-state corrections using magnitude-only compute-uncompute measurements. Analytical variance estimates predict 1.6 mHa precision with a few hundred shots, about 100 times fewer than the single-determinant formulation and three orders of magnitude fewer than optimized Hamiltonian-term sampling. Ground-State Sampling Correspondence between Penalty-Based and Constrained Quantum Annealing

Quantum annealing generally samples degenerate ground states with nonuniform probabilities. This unfair sampling depends on how the driver Hamiltonian couples these states near the end of annealing. For combinatorial optimization problems with a cardinality constraint, the penalty method and constrained quantum annealing (CQA) differ in these couplings. The transverse-field driver of the penalty method does not directly couple feasible ground states, whereas the fully connected XY driver of CQA couples pairs of them related by a single spin exchange. We investigate how this difference affects ground-state sampling, using closed-system simulations of random graph bipartitioning instances together with degenerate perturbation theory. For the instances studied, the conditional ground-state distribution of penalty-based quantum annealing approaches that of CQA at sufficiently large penalties and long annealing times. This convergence can be explained by the effective Hamiltonian of penalty-based quantum annealing, in which virtual single-spin flips to infeasible states generate a second-order interaction proportional to the CQA driver projected onto the feasible ground-state manifold, which is the leading term of the effective Hamiltonian of CQA. In the numerical simulations, the Jensen--Shannon divergence between the conditional ground-state distributions of penalty-based quantum annealing and CQA decreases approximately as the inverse square of the penalty-to-objective energy ratio, consistent with the perturbative analysis. This correspondence between the effective Hamiltonians extends to equal-weight fixed-magnetization constraints under the same perturbative conditions, providing a common mechanism for sampling bias in the two implementations.

Average lower bound on magnetic double-well tunneling with asymptotically large well separation

We study tunneling between two identical, smooth, compactly supported potential wells in a constant perpendicular magnetic field, with the coupling and magnetic field fixed and the well separation tending to infinity. For a sufficiently small positive magnetic field, we prove lower bounds on the hopping coefficient and on the splitting of the two lowest eigenvalues outside a set of separations of density zero. If the well centers are separated by $2d_1$ and the magnetic field strength is $bλ$, the lower bounds have the form $e^{-bλd_1^2-Cd_1^{1+δ}}$ for every fixed $δ\in(0,1)$. No radial or reflection symmetry of the single-well potential is assumed. The proof combines the explicit Landau resolvent kernel with a complex analytic estimate for logarithmic averages of the hopping coefficient.

Non-Markovian exceptional points and information backflow: a Heisenberg reaction-coordinate approach

The primary pathway to the investigation of exceptional points (EPs) in open quantum systems has been the use of Markovian master equations. However, recently it has been realised that they can arise in settings that show non-Markovian behaviour. The main approach in this direction has been the use of pseudomodes, which allow the influence of a structured environment to be replaced by new damped auxiliary modes. In this work, we use a Heisenberg-equation approach which relies on the reaction-coordinate formalism to obtain exact analytical dynamics. First, we show how our method considerably simplifies the analysis compared to the use of master equations. Second, we utilise it to discuss non-Markovian revivals in systems with second and higher-order EPs, showing that the notion that EPs demarcate the onset of revivals is not generally true. We then utilise our results to discuss the dynamical effects of higher-order non-Markovian EPs, deriving bounds on the number of revivals and investigating enhanced asymptotic relaxation near higher-order EPs. Finally, we investigate non-Markovian EPs with engineered reservoirs and show how they can be utilised to control the rate of irreversible information loss.

Beyond Fidelity: Resolving Errors in Photonic Seed State Generation

An important aspect of many linear optical quantum computing architectures is probabilistically generating small dual-rail qubit entangled states, or seed states. While many schemes to achieve this have been discovered, attention has been primarily focused on minimizing input resources and maximizing success probability rather than establishing a detailed understanding of linear-optical error performance. In this work, we develop a framework for resolving sensitivities to physically distinct errors in linear optical state generation, allowing us to identify precise ways in which these errors affect the properties of the output state. We use this framework to identify seed state generators in which destructive many-photon interference prevents detector loss and detector noise from causing a falsely successful heralding event. We show that this effect suppresses errors, for example cutting the effective loss on the output of particular Bell state generators in half relative to a standard scheme. We also present a new Bell state generator in which both detector loss and detector noise do not affect the quality of the output Bell state to first order, and a 4GHZ generator with >75% higher success probability than a standard circuit. Local Measurement Theory for Quantum Cellular Automata

The formulation of quantum measurements compatible with relativistic causality is obstructed by the tension between the locality of observable algebras and the dynamical state transformations induced by measurement processes. We develop a local measurement theory for quantum cellular automata, lattices of finite-dimensional quantum systems with a causal reversible dynamics evolving in discrete time steps. Measurement processes are described by localized completely positive maps and organized into operational measurement protocols, finite non-adaptive sequences of local instruments distributed across the causal cone induced by the automaton local dynamics. Each protocol induces an effective measurement on the initial time slice, whose support is constrained by the causal structure generated by the automaton. Non-local measurement effects therefore arise through causal propagation and composition of local operations, rather than from primitive non-local interventions or instantaneous collapse prescriptions. We establish the corresponding spatial support bounds and characterize the resulting measurement architecture. As an explicit example, we construct a two-step protocol for a one-dimensional nearest-neighbour qubit automaton which, for suitable automaton parameters, realizes a Bell-basis projective measurement on two neighbouring sites after coarse-graining of the local outcomes. Away from this point, the same protocol generates a continuous family of deformed Bell measurements whose entanglement and stabilizer Rényi non-classicality can be quantified explicitly. The framework thus provides a causally consistent operator-algebraic mechanism for synthesizing spatially extended quantum measurements from strictly local operations.

Exact projective-steering boundary and sample-size optimization for angularly averaged hyperon-antihyperon states

In hyperon-antihyperon production through electron-positron annihilation, quantum steering provides a stricter test of nonclassical spin correlations than entanglement. We determine when steering survives angular averaging and optimize angular selections to reduce the sample size needed to resolve it. In the specified production-frame spin convention, we show that windows symmetric about 90 degrees with even production-angle acceptance yield phase-independent Bell-diagonal states. Applying the necessary and sufficient criterion for steering under arbitrary dichotomic projective measurements determines the exact boundary for this family and reveals a steering region missed by the three-setting Cavalcanti-Jones-Wiseman-Reid (CJWR) criterion. For positive production parameters and uniform full-range acceptance, the steering threshold is $α\simeq0.36065$. We derive the covariance of weak-decay angular moments, construct fixed linear witnesses, and optimize the angular window. For $J/ψ\toΛ\barΛ$, steering survives full-range averaging. The statistical model predicts that, after separately optimizing the two angular selections at nominal $z=5$, the required full-range pair sample decreases from $8.61\times10^4$ for CJWR to $2.80\times10^4$ for the projective witness, a 67.4% reduction. This advantage persists under the production-acceptance and analyzing-power variations studied.

Benchmarking the Quantum Leap: Auditing LLM Evaluation for Quantum Code Generation

Large language models (LLMs) can generate quantum programs, but reliable evaluation remains difficult because benchmarks differ in task composition, scoring, and failure handling. We evaluate 15 hosted frontier, open-weight, quantum-specialized, and retrieval-augmented models on five quantum code-generation suites (530 tasks), plus 10 models on our new QuantumBenchEval (QBE) benchmark (96 tasks across six topics). We report bootstrap confidence intervals, multiplicity-adjusted paired comparisons, and audits of output validity and evaluator behavior. QiskitHumanEval-Standard approaches saturation (98.7% pass@1), while its Hard variant retains headroom (63.8%). None of the five top-two comparisons across primary suites remains significant after multiple-comparison correction. Evaluator design substantially changes scores: removing checks for unspecified quantities raises GPT-6 Astra's QBE pass@1 from 0% to 94.1% (T1) and from 1.2% to 92.9% (T6), without changing model outputs. We identify 17 QuanBench-117 tasks with shared failures linked to benchmark-owned imports. Controlled probes also reveal unexecuted unit tests, leakage of canonical functions into candidate execution, and reference imports that block otherwise valid candidates. These defects can produce both false failures and false passes, so historical QuanBench results require revalidation under an isolated evaluator. Overall, leaderboard scores alone do not adequately characterize progress in quantum code generation. We release our harness, per-sample results, and audit artifacts and recommend validating evaluators, isolating candidate code, and reporting uncertainty and output validity.

Exploring ground state phase diagrams of Rydberg atom arrays with finite projected entangled-pair states

Rydberg atom arrays (RAA) have been advocated as a promising avenue for the quantum simulation of different exotic quantum states. Numerical predictions for the ground state properties of this experimental platform can, however, be challenging in two-dimensional setups. In particular, faithfully treating long-range interactions is often crucial for distinguishing the fate of competing phases, but it usually comes at considerable cost. Projected entangled-pair states (PEPS), optimized via a sampling-based approach, have been shown to deal with such terms on the same ground as shorter-range ones. In this work, we employ these tools to investigate debated physical questions in different frustrated geometries of RAA, from the emergence of spin-glass physics on the Kagomé lattice to topological order beyond the PXP model on the Ruby lattice. Majorization for superpositions of nonorthogonal states and the existence of golden states

In the resource theory of superposition relative to linearly independent, nonorthogonal reference states, golden states are known for two reference states and for special overlap matrices, and all of them are maximally coherent in the Löwdin basis. We clarify what decides their existence and their Löwdin structure. The robustness and the geometric measure of superposition are maximized by the same states, which overlap equally with every reference state; their maximal values are set by the ground-state energy of a classical $XY$ model on the overlaps of the dual basis. For every overlap matrix, these states, and only these, are golden under the maximal superposition-free operations, but they need not be maximally coherent in the Löwdin basis. For the Kraus-type free operations, we prove a necessary and sufficient criterion, Gram majorization, for converting pure states into targets with nonzero coefficients; it reduces to majorization for orthonormal reference states. It shows that golden states of these operations must be maximally coherent in the Löwdin basis, but that this does not ensure their existence: three reference states that are not orthonormal admit a golden state if and only if their overlaps have a common modulus $s$ and their Bargmann phase $Φ$ obeys $s\geq2\cos[(π+|Φ|)/3]$. Equal-overlap states also yield a complete family of superposition witnesses in the Löwdin basis, which show that white noise on the span of the reference states is a resource unless these states are orthonormal.

Observation and control of polariton noise in a thermal Rydberg atom electrometer

The establishment of metrics and control of fundamental noise sources in quantum sensing is a long-standing goal in quantum science and technology. We report on the observation of polariton-mediated optical noise in a thermal Rydberg atom vapor and demonstrate its active control and suppression using a radio-frequency field in Rydberg electrometry. The noise exhibits structured amplification and reduction across the Rydberg electromagnetically induced transparency resonance. This reveals a nonlinear slow-light feedback mechanism in which the polariton medium stores and releases probe-field energy. We show that the reference radio-frequency field used in Rydberg heterodyne receivers can also tune the medium into an operating regime where high electromagnetic-field responsivity coincides with reduced optical noise. This operating-point control enables optimization of the electric-field sensitivity within the constraints imposed by Rydberg-polariton physics. These results establish polariton noise as a previously unrecognized intrinsic noise source in thermal Rydberg vapors and the corresponding control method as a crucial aspect of continued efforts to achieve fundamental sensitivity limits in Rydberg electrometry.

KrMM Recurrences: HPC-Enhanced Estimators for Nonlinear Classical Shadows

While quantum technologies are rapidly evolving, near-term applications heavily rely on hybrid quantum-classical paradigms. Among these, the classical shadows framework has gained a lot of traction, not only for quantum information science, but also for the development and verification of quantum hardware. It allows to efficiently classically estimate many properties of quantum states from randomized measurements alone, a task that is otherwise intractable due to the destructive nature of measurements in quantum systems. There are, however, many properties of interest that are, due to their very problem formulation, not efficiently estimable. In this work, we identify a computational kernel occurring in estimators of polynomial functions, a class of problems that are in general computationally difficult to solve. We show that this structure allows a row-wise partition that, together with a custom SIMD vectorization, enables massive parallelization and can reduce the computational cost significantly. We show applicability and scalability of the approach on different tasks, and our results showcase that the approach allows high sequential throughput and close-to-ideal parallel scaling. The work thus provides a new way of identifying and exploiting structure in classical shadow estimators, resulting in practical advantages on inherently difficult problems.

End-to-End Optimization of Partial Fault Tolerance in Neutral-Atom Arrays

Fault-tolerant (FT) quantum computation offers a pathway to quantum advantage, but its realization is hindered by its significant overhead. The partially FT "STAR" architecture reduces the cost of universal computation at the price of relaxing FT requirements for non-Clifford operations, increasing exposure to hardware noise. Therefore, maximizing its performance and applicability demands careful co-design with the underlying hardware. In this work we investigate the co-design of the STAR architecture with neutral-atom platforms, connecting the underlying atomic physics to logical error modeling and application-level performance. At the physical level, we identify a favorable noise bias in $^{171}\mathrm{Yb}$ Rydberg gates, and show how it can be enhanced through error-selective pulse optimization. At the logical level, we identify an intrinsic error suppression mechanism, yielding vanishing logical coherent noise in leading order. Detailed circuit-level simulations of surface code implementations show more than an order of magnitude reduction in logical error relative to previous estimates. As a concrete target application, we focus on the Fermi-Hubbard model with periodic boundaries, for which we design a simulation circuit particularly suited to early implementations. We quantify the resource tradeoff of partially and fully FT approaches to gate synthesis, with the former showing approximately an order of magnitude reduction in state-injection depth, and an even larger reduction in Clifford resources. Comparing with classical and fully FT approaches, we identify regimes where partial fault tolerance provides both lower overheads and lower error rates. In our estimates, this is accessible with moderate error rates $p\sim 10^{-3}$ and a few thousand qubits, underlining that for these types of problems, partial FT approaches provide a compelling pathway to early quantum advantage.

The Four-Qubit Hyperbolic Quadric Has Contextuality Degree 315

The contextuality degree of a set of Pauli contexts is the least number of contexts that every noncontextual $\pm1$ assignment violates. For the four-qubit hyperbolic quadric $\mathcal{Q}^{+}(7,2)$, with $135$ points and $1575$ lines as contexts, Muller et al. conjectured from heuristic searches that the degree is $315$ and that the violated contexts of every optimal assignment form a dual polar space $DW(5,2)$. We prove both conjectures. The lower bound has a short proof by hand: every doily ($GQ(2,2)$) of the quadric contains ten Peres-Mermin grids covering each of its lines four times, so every assignment violates at least three of its fifteen lines, and averaging over the doilies gives $1575/5=315$. The equality case is local: an assignment is optimal exactly when it violates one ruling of one grid in every doily. Using this as a SAT encoding, we show that there are exactly $240$ optimal violated sets, one orbit of $\mathrm{O}^+(8,2)$, each isomorphic to $DW(5,2)$. The same argument gives the first lower bounds $\lceil L/5\rceil$ for all quadrics with $L$ lines in $N\ge4$ qubits. The degree theorem is kernel-checked in Lean 4, and the completeness of the enumeration is certified by a DRAT proof checked by the verified checker cake_lpr.

We examined the energy-momentum relationship of the valence band of Cu$_2$O, which has gained interest as an emerging solid host for giant Rydberg states. Our findings reveal a nonparabolic dispersion, described using the rest mass (0.570 times the free electron mass) and rescaled light speed ($1.6\times 10^5$ m/s), which draws parallels with relativistic theory for high-energy particles. We propose a straightforward expression for the bound-state energies of Rydberg excitons with finite quantum defects, whose energy scheme deviates from the hydrogenic series. This formulation identifies relativistic kinematics as the physical origin of quantum defects, offering a streamlined basis for future studies on complex many-body correlations.

Higher-order Parity Constraints for Quantum Approximate Optimization

The Parity Architecture was originally introduced in the context of quantum optimization algorithms to overcome hardware connectivity constraints. Here, we show that its benefits extend beyond this role by identifying specific parity transformations that enhance the success probability of the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) for optimization problems defined on hardware-native and sparse graphs. Numerical simulations suggest that this enhancement originates from the higher-order operators introduced by the Parity representation, which accelerate the propagation of correlations across the physical device. We investigate this mechanism for the Signed Max-Cut problem on 3-regular graph instances and demonstrate its practical relevance on IBM Heron quantum processors. High-order resonances and emergent coherent dynamics in interacting Stark chains

A linear potential gradient can strongly suppress quantum dynamics, while interactions can activate resonant many-body processes whose higher-order structure remains largely unexplored. Here we uncover a systematic hierarchy of high-order interaction--tilt resonances in an interacting Stark chain, where the resonance order counts the minimal number of elementary exchange steps involved. These resonances manifest as long-lived coherent oscillations with order-dependent timescales. By isolating individual resonant channels and developing a microscopic effective description, we connect these dynamical timescales to the underlying many-body exchange processes and reveal a pronounced dependence on the initial-state structure. Our results provide crucial insights into high-order many-body resonances and show how commensurate interaction-to-tilt ratios generate a hierarchy of parametrically slow coherent many-body dynamics.

Squeezing-type lower bounds on generalized metrological sensitivity

We introduce squeezing-type lower bounds for a hierarchy of variance bounds under specific unbiasedness constraints, which generalizes the quantum Cramér-Rao bound. Our construction directly extends standard squeezing coefficients to the corresponding generalized metrological sensitivities while retaining their experimentally accessible structure. We show how observables maximizing these bounds can be identified analytically within families of accessible observables. Universal Quantum Criticality from a Gaussian Variational Manifold

Universal behavior at continuous phase transitions is encoded in long-wavelength fixed-point theories controlled by dimensionality and symmetries. It is a central question for which systems this universal behavior and the underlying long-wavelength description can be captured by a noninteracting theory allowing for analytical and accurate predictions. In this work we provide a systematic procedure for obtaining the best noninteracting fermionic description of a quantum phase transition through fermionic Gaussian states. We demonstrate the performance of our procedure for the interacting long-range transverse-field Ising chain. This formulation reduces the problem to a self-consistent Euler equation that yields a critical line in close agreement with independent numerical benchmarks, while its singular momentum structure provides analytic predictions for continuously varying critical properties. Yet exact diagonalization shows that Gaussian-state fidelity to the exact ground state is poorest, and non-Gaussianity strongest, near the transition. Thus, accurate predictions for critical properties can emerge from the Gaussian variational description even where its microscopic wave-function approximation is weakest.

Q-MALDetect: Quantum Image-Based Malware Detection and Adversarial Robustness Analysis

Quantum machine-learning (QML) models are increasingly considered for security applications, yet their adversarial robustness in malware analysis remains insufficiently understood. This paper presents Q-MALDetect, a hybrid convolutional and variational-quantum detector for byte-image representations of x86-64 Executable and Linkable Format (ELF) binaries, and evaluates it under feature-space and a range of Grad-CAM-guided, executable-aware evasion attacks. The model achieves 97.2% training accuracy, but its robustness deteriorates as attack strength increases, with the strongest evaluated feature-space attack reaching a 78.7% attack success rate (ASR). Among executable-aware attacks, Grad-CAM-guided no-operation (NOP) insertion produces the highest conditional ASR of 32.9%. Dynamic adversarial training is the most effective evaluated defense, reducing feature-space ASR to as low as 5.0%, executable-aware ASR to 7.7%, and the sequential-attack ASR to 34.0%. In contrast, convolutional-autoencoder preprocessing increases executable-aware ASR to 39.7%, coinciding with a rise in Grad-CAM attribution mass within the mutable .text region from 35.2% to 45.7%. Ablation results reveal no monotonic relationship between qubit count and robustness, while the encoding interface modifies the learned attack surface without neutralizing the strongest attack. These findings demonstrate that the effectiveness of attacks and defenses depends strongly on the perturbation constraints and evaluation conditions. Reliable deployment of QML malware detectors therefore requires assessment against both feature-space and executable-aware attacks rather than reliance on clean accuracy or generic denoising alone.

Strong-to-Weak Spontaneous Symmetry Breaking Transitions on a Quantum Processor

Mixed quantum states can exhibit unconventional forms of symmetry breaking without pure-state counterparts. A prominent example is strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SW-SSB), which cannot be diagnosed by observables linear in the density matrix. Here, we experimentally resolve finite-size critical signatures of SW-SSB transitions on a superconducting quantum processor. We develop a measurement protocol that combines quantum measurements with classical processing based on the known dephasing channel to reconstruct nonlinear mixed-state correlators. Using only two measurement settings, the protocol reconstructs fidelity, Rényi-2, and linear correlators, as well as string observables. Applying this approach to matrix-product states subjected to power-law-correlated dephasing, we observe finite-size crossings and scaling collapses in nonlinear correlators, accompanied by decaying linear correlations. We further map the estimated SW-SSB transition points across initial-state and channel parameters. Measurements of linear and Rényi-2 string observables further reveal signatures of mixed-state symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases, with a nonlinear string-order crossover aligned with an independently estimated SW-SSB transition point. Our results establish a quantum-classical approach to probing SW-SSB critical behavior and mixed-state SPT order.
